| **[Autonomous Mobile Robots on a Computing Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16652v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce CABOT, a computational model for autonomous mobile robots in which the discrete plane the robots operate on is not a passive stage but an active computing medium. In the classical Look-Compute-Move (LCM) model every increase in computational capability is obtained by strengthening the robots themselves, with lights, memory or wider visibility, at a cost paid once per robot. CABOT strengthens the environment instead: each cell of the grid holds a state from a finite set Q, the states evolve synchronously under a uniform local rule that also senses whether a robot stands on the cell, that is, the floor is a cellular automaton, and the Look operation returns the states of the visible cells in addition to the positions of the visible robots. The robots are as weak as we can make them: oblivious, silent, lightless, myopic with visibility range 1, without multiplicity detection and unable to write to the floor. The number of field states |Q|, rather than any robot capability, becomes the measure of the resources required. On an inert floor (|Q| = 1) such robots can neither form a line, nor gather from scattered positions, nor explore a grid. We show that a handful of states changes this. Line Formation from connected configurations is solvable with four states, and field-silently, so that the floor also reaches a fixed point, with five. Gathering from arbitrary, possibly disconnected, configurations is solvable with three states and Line Formation from arbitrary configurations with six; we prove that neither can be solved field-silently with any number of states. A single robot explores every finite grid forever with four states and every torus with two, which is optimal. Further rule sets, validated by simulation only, equalize the spacing of robots on a row, fill a pattern painted on the floor and guide robots through a maze, and indicate the range of the model.</p></details> | 19 pages, 5 figures |
| **[A Marine Debris Detection Framework for Ocean Robots via Self-Attention Enhancement and Feature Interaction Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07388v3)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Marine debris detection for ocean robot is crucial for ecological protection, yet performance is often degraded by low-quality images with blur, complex backgrounds, and small targets. Additionally, a lightweight shift-based operation is introduced to enhance fine-grained feature extraction for objects of varying scales while maintaining parameter efficiency. We further propose SFG-Loss to mitigate class imbalance and optimization instability via dynamic sample reweighting. Experiments on the UODM dataset demonstrate that YOLO-MD achieves 0.875 precision, 0.822 F1-score, and 0.849 mAP50, outperforming the latest state-of-the-art methods. The effectiveness of this method has also been verified through real-world robotic edge deployment experiments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Langu...</summary><p>Language polishing only; no substantive changes to content, data, results, or conclusions</p></details> |
| **[Reins: Frontier Multimodal Models Driving Real Robot Arms on Long-Horizon Tasks, Zero-Shot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16632v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier models such as GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 can now plan and call tools, yet they still struggle to drive real robots through long-horizon, contact-rich manipulation, blind while the arm moves and while they think. The model chains actions in one call, each with a condition that must hold, stopping at the first that fails; a second instance, the narrator, watches the cameras continuously and reports what changed with every result. From images, Reins also builds a simulation-ready twin of the scene for continual policy learning. With natural-language prompts and no task-specific code, GPT-6.1 Sol succeeds in 88% of real episodes on five tasks; without the harness, it fails every shell game, taking 8 times as long. Three frontier models perform alike but differ in tracking a scene that changes while the actor thinks. Code will be released upon publication.</p></details> |  |
| **[Robots That Talk Too Much and Don't Listen Enough](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16473v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Until recently, the biggest obstacles to natural spoken interaction with social robots were speech recognition errors and turn-taking failures. | **[ManiLadder: Benchmarking Robot Manipulation Through a Categorized and Multi-Difficulty Level Task Ladder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16374v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in robotic manipulation have enabled increasingly complex and dexterous behaviors, yet existing benchmarks still focus on simple pick-and-place tasks and lack a systematic framework for measuring progress in robot learning along the axis of task difficulty. ManiLadder contains 112 carefully designed tasks stratified into 4 difficulty levels, with 8 categories in each level, spanning rigid-body and deformable-object manipulation, single-arm and dual-arm settings, and gripper and dexterous-hand end-effectors. To calibrate difficulty, we fix the data amount the same for every task and use a unified flow- matching imitation learning policy under the same training and evaluation protocols, then use its evaluation success rate as an operational empirical difficulty signal. All tasks are iteratively refined until they fall into target difficulty ranges, which are then verified across different random seeds, policies, and agentic learning systems. We further analyze the task properties, compare different policy performances and training settings, analyze task transfer and data scaling behaviors, and conduct real-world experiments showing that the designed tasks preserve their difficulty ordering beyond simulation. ManiLad- der provides a calibrated ladder for measuring, comparing, and advancing robotic manipulation capabilities. | **[Compute Regeneration through Re-Productization: Reorganizing Retired Edge AI Silicon into a Delivered Multilingual Service Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11698v2)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Useful edge hardware can lose product support while retaining substantial computation. A source-grounded inventory shows the box alone supplies inference, while the combined system exposes five on-device AI services, a seven-language speech chain, three sensing domains, and one deterministic seven-byte control boundary. AI and orchestration stay on the Xavier; finite motor-pulse generation on an STM32F407. Audits cover the six-mode control state machine, readiness barriers and sound orientation. Historical single-prototype measurements report 1,782 MB GPU memory with YOLO, Whisper-small, and NLLB-200 resident, a direct-control p99 deviation rising from 0.24 to 49.93 ms under concurrent AI load, and six of six trigger-to-follow demonstrations at 4.35 s mean latency, with conversational voice unavailable. A November campaign restores an isolated inference stack, re-measures AI workloads, and repeats control-frame runs. Driving two execution organisations with the same 6,000 measured 50 ms frame intervals shows the finite-pulse boundary removes host jitter from the pulse train but not the command start phase; its stopping bound is host-enforced, while the MCU 16-bit pulse field is unclamped. A six-check static audit exposes a host--MCU queue mismatch, and a 14-scenario replay executes the audited main() dispatcher across six modes with zero physical device opens and 54 contract-checked frames. | **[Alloy: A Multi-Material Physics Engine for Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16312v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robots handle much more than rigid objects: towels, cables, water, sand. Each object is described once; Alloy assigns it to a solver suited to its material, couples the solvers inside every control step, and runs the scene in many parallel worlds on one GPU, or on a CPU. Every world carries a health grade, to help tell a failure of the physics from a failure of the policy. Alloy is built on Newton, MuJoCo Warp and Warp, and it is written to be driven by coding agents: its documentation ships inside the package, every error names its fix, and the engine is exposed as tools over the Model Context Protocol. In a cold test, agents that had never seen Alloy installed a pre-release from the package index and trained policies for walking, cable handling and pouring on a single GPU. We also report how throughput grows with the number of worlds, depth-camera throughput, agreement with closed-form references, and a reliability test over a thousand worlds.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 4 figures, 4 tables. | **[Robot Navigation and Obstacle Avoidance from Randomly Positioned External Cameras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16282v1)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study robot navigation in indoor environments from fixed exocentric cameras (infrastructure-mounted, external to the robot). To address this, we propose Viewpoint-Invariant Navigation from Exocentric Cameras (VINE). VINE uses an obstacle map estimation network, trained with supervised learning, that takes exocentric images, camera pose, robot pose, and room dimensions as input and produces a viewpoint-invariant obstacle map. This map can be used either by a PPO policy or by a planner such as A*. VINE's map estimation performs better than perspective mapping approaches, handles cameras placed across a wide range of positions and orientations without view-specific tuning, requires only approximate camera pose estimates (noise with a standard deviation of 30cm per axis in x, y, and z and about 20° of rotation error on average), is robust to intrinsic variation, and extends to two-camera setups with partial coverage. | **[EmbodiedRSI: Active Continual Robot Learning Through Hypothesis-Guided Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10498v2)** | 2026-11-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robot foundation models provide strong visuomotor control, yet their performance can degrade when object positions or task instructions change. The Slow System builds Hierarchical Memory, and Reward-Grounded Memory Learning selects effective memory according to their value for later Fast-System improvement. On RoboCasa365, EmbodiedRSI reaches 77.0% overall success and 71.3% on Composite-Unseen, compared with 40.1% for the best baseline. EmbodiedRSI also reaches 86.8% overall success on LIBERO-Pro. We present Robo-SWE, a dataset of 236 executable issue-resolution tasks mined from merged pull requests in 28 public robotics repositories in four domains: ROS 2 systems, simulation and physics engines, robotics algorithm libraries, and robot-learning frameworks. Each task contains the original issue text, a container recipe for the repository at the commit before the fix, the developers' fix, and the developers' tests, which fail before the fix and pass after it in repeated offline runs. The dataset also keeps a provenance ledger of all 1,567 candidate pull requests, with the stage each reached and the reason for every rejection, and, as a first use, 3,540 graded attempts by five coding-agent setups with recorded patches and 3,529 available trajectories. | **[Discrete Model-Based Diffusion for Combinatorial Optimization in Multi-Robot Task Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13382v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Decision making in multi-robot systems often involves discrete decision variables, leading to NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems that must be solved within seconds for near real-time implementation. Denoising diffusion has emerged as a powerful tool for such problems, but existing approaches face a trade-off: Model-Based Diffusion (MBD) incorporates diffusion in sampling-based optimization (SBO) without training but only over continuous spaces, while existing diffusion-based combinatorial solvers operate over discrete spaces but require offline training. To this end, we propose Discrete Model-Based Diffusion (Discrete MBD), a training-free SBO method that directly optimizes discrete variables by approximating categorical reverse transition distributions through Monte Carlo sampling. We establish a finite-step lower bound on the probability of finding a global minimizer and show that this bound converges to one as the number of denoising steps increases. Extensive experiments on generic integer programs and heterogeneous multi-robot coalition formation and scheduling demonstrate that Discrete MBD outperforms SBO baselines in nearly all settings while achieving solution quality competitive with that of mixed-integer programming and reinforcement learning methods at a fraction of their computational cost.</p></details> | 11 pages, 3 figures |

| **[Intent Representation and Human Distraction Modulate the Effects of Legible Robot Motion in Hallway Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40158v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Legibility in human-robot interaction (HRI) is often described as the property of robot motion that enables an observer to confidently and quickly infer the robot's intent. Mature frameworks exist for generating legible motion in front of static observers, typically by making the robot's destination apparent as early as possible. Social robot navigation in public spaces, however, presents a different challenge: pedestrians are themselves in motion, often with divided attention, and must coordinate with the robot in real time. In such encounters, where the robot is ultimately headed may matter less to a pedestrian than how it intends to pass them. This raises two questions: which aspect of intent should legible motion convey, and does legibility remain beneficial when pedestrians are not fully attentive to the robot? We address these questions in the ubiquitous and demanding scenario of hallway navigation, through two controlled user studies comparing multiple legibility formulations implemented within a shared model predictive control framework. Study 1 (N = 45) compares destination-based and passing-side intent representations, showing that the latter, particularly when adaptively updated, allows for smoother human motion and is perceived as more competent and less mentally and physically demanding than the former. Second, deleting semantic relations costs 15.3 points of strict belief-state accuracy on relation-dependent scenarios (Holm-corrected p<0.001), but an information-matched structured list outperforms the full graph and shuffling the graph does not hurt: relation content matters where required, while graph serialization confers no consistent advantage. Third, belief accuracy is strongly non-monotonic along the evidence sequence, with mid-trajectory failures that endpoint-only evaluation cannot detect. All scenarios, references, prompts, metrics, and analysis code are released for offline, hardware-free reproduction.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages (10 main + 5 supplementary), 6 figures, 4 tables; supplement adds 1 figure and 7 tables. Project page: https://creg-bench.github.io</p></details> |
| **[DreamTrue: Action-Faithful Robot World Model with Counterfactual Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12468v2)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present DreamTrue, a multi-view, cross-embodiment robot world model for action-faithful and physically plausible video prediction. Training such a model on existing robot datasets faces two obstacles: imprecise calibration can impair action following, while limited coverage of unsuccessful interactions can bias predictions toward successful outcomes. To improve action following across embodiments, we render action trajectories into image-space conditions and introduce offline geometric calibration to align these conditions with the target videos. To broaden interaction coverage, we introduce counterfactual post-training, modifying recorded action trajectories and generating future videos under a wider range of actions and contact configurations. To provide feedback on these predictions without paired ground-truth futures, we construct a human-annotated video dataset covering robot, object, and interaction defects and use it to train an embodied video reward model. Its scores guide reinforcement-learning post-training toward more physically plausible interaction outcomes. On AgiBot, DreamTrue attains state-of-the-art action following, while reducing the human-assessed interaction defect rate from from 48.12% to 6.25%. Notably, our model ranks first in the world model track of the AgiBot World Challenge 2026. The project page can be found at https://brave-eai.github.io/DreamTrue.</p></details> | <details><summary>proje...</summary><p>project page: https://brave-eai.github.io/DreamTrue; code: https://github.com/brave-eai/DreamTrue</p></details> |
| **[Budget-aware Correspondence-driven View Selection for Real-time 3D Perception of Mobile Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11809v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D visual perception and scene reconstruction are critical for autonomous robots and intelligent transportation, yet real-world deployments often generate long, redundant image sequences that hinder downstream processing. This report investigates BCVS (budget-aware correspondence-driven view sampling), a pose-free view selection algorithm that offers 22+ FPS on a CPU, enabling online filtering for efficient 3D perception by mobile robots. Unlike existing approaches that rely on full structure-from-motion (SfM) pipelines or camera pose estimation, BCVS employs a purely correspondence-based scoring strategy that prioritizes view novelty while maintaining sufficient overlap for geometric stability. Given a user-specified budget, BCVS iteratively selects the most distinct frames and suppresses near-duplicates, producing compact subsets that preserve connectivity and minimize reconstruction loss. Extensive experiments on benchmark show that BCVS outperforms CPU-friendly baselines at tight budgets and remains competitive as budgets scale. Field trials with large aerial and marine robotics data further validate its practicality and scalability: BCVS reduces redundancy up to 16% while sustaining robust SfM registration and high-quality 3D reconstructions.</p></details> | 12 pages |
| **[HolidayFactory 1.0: Simulating, Generating, and Evaluating Industrial Robot Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11789v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robots are typically evaluated in episodes that begin from a reset, while industrial work consists of repeated jobs whose outcomes affect subsequent operations. Evaluating such work at scale requires simulation of task-critical physical interactions, construction of industrial environments, and measurements of performance as scene state persists. We present HOLIDAYFACTORY 1.0, a framework for simulating, generating, and evaluating industrial robot work. Given a human-specified workplace and task, the framework defines a world pairing a scenario of scene variants and process logic with an evaluation defining a job, metrics, and a rubric. HOLIDAYFACTORY 1.0 comprises three components. HOLIDAY-SIM extends Newton's SDF-based hydroelastic contact with additional constitutive laws and a semi-implicit primal-dual solver, couples stateful actuator dynamics to articulated motion, and derives tactile and joint observations from simulated states. Agentic world construction generates simulation-ready assets and scene variants using geometric and interaction checks, and proposes task-conditioned evaluations for human confirmation and executable checks. 24/7-BENCH introduces seven industrial tasks in which jobs are evaluated without resetting the scene, measuring completed, failed, and unfinished jobs, throughput, latency, and motor energy. Controlled studies characterize the proposed contact, actuator, and tactile models and examine the effectiveness of verification-guided world construction. Experiments on scene and evaluation generation further reveal the limitations of model critics and the difficulty of ensuring that generated evaluations faithfully capture task requirements. We examine job-level evaluations with scripted trajectories (e.g., code-as-policy) but have not yet compared policies over persistent runs.</p></details> |  |
